  A `Protocol` (`Invocable`) lets tools and procedures share **the same** argument validation, confirmation card and permission gate: duplicating them would have created two validations that diverge on the first change, offering or denying acts depending on which door the call came through.
  170 mcp-server tests (11 new), 79 gateway tests (4 new routes), 430 document-service tests, 1,937 frontend tests, lint green across the 11 services. Mutated separately and red with their test: the terceros route removed from the gateway, a broken step link passing `None`, and a report that hides what was completed.
  *Why*: without a populated database the whole system cannot be tested, and an MCP offering only isolated calls forces the model to invent the procedure — *Impact*: new `scripts/seed_corpus.py`, `/api/v1/terceros` routed in the gateway, four MCP procedures with their runner and their step report, exposed over both transports (REST and the MCP protocol).

- `fix(archive,storage,frontend)`: **two capabilities built that the screen could not reach** — the per-document hash of the electronic index (E15 T-08), and blind group administration
  From the pending-items review the user asked for. Of the three noted, **the first was not a defect**: checked against the running stack, an invalid token, one with a forged signature and a missing header all three return the correct `401` — the gateway's `503` fires only when auth-service returns 5xx or does not answer, which is the right semantics. I had taken it as open by reading the `!= 200` branch and an old note, without testing it.
  **1) The electronic index carried no hash for anything linked from the interface.** RT-15 art. 4.3.2.3 requires it, and the system has ALWAYS had it: storage-service computes the SHA-256 on upload. What was missing was someone to fetch it — the link proxy sends `radicado_id` and `tracking_number`, so `content_hash` stayed null. Result: "documents with a verified hash: 0 / N" on expedientes whose radicados did have files with a hash sitting right there.
  **It is not asked of the client**, for the same reason the department is derived from the municipality: a hash supplied by whoever links proves nothing about what the archive holds. The server resolves it —the radicado's annexes from document-service, their SHA-256 from storage— and the contract field remains for callers who know it first-hand (import with fixity, E11 INT-05): **what is declared wins over what is resolved**.
  **What "the hash of a document" means when it carries several annexes is declared in one place** (`app/core/huella_documento.py`): no annexes, no hash, and it is SAID (`sin_huella_declarada`, which is not an integrity discrepancy); one annex, its SHA-256 as is; several, the SHA-256 of their **sorted** hashes — a one-level Merkle tree. Sorting is what makes the hash a function of the set rather than of the order the database returned the rows: without it the same evidence would yield two values and verification would report an alteration that never happened.
  **Best-effort on purpose**: if document-service or storage do not answer, the link still happens, without a hash. Linking a radicado to its expediente is an archival act that cannot be held hostage to two other services being up, and a document with no hash is something the index knows how to declare; aborting would trade a missing datum for lost work. Resolution happens **outside the transaction**: that stretch is 100% DB so the tenant's `pg_advisory_xact_lock` is not held across a network round trip.
  **2) `/admin/grupos` administered blind, and the backend did expose the reads.** The drawer added and removed members, set permissions and clearance without showing any of the stored state: instead it displayed a "this session" list of what it had itself done — the memory of the clicks, not the membership. All three `GET`s had existed in auth-service since the RBAC read mirror. What kept the hole open was **a comment claiming they did not exist**, repeated in the proxy, in `+page.server.js` and in the screen's own notice. Fourth time in this project that a false sentence in a comment is the vector: deleting it is part of the fix.
  A concrete consequence: since every permission selector showed 0, saving any one of them could **silently downgrade** another already granted at level 3.
  Verified in the browser against `tenant_demo`: linking `2026-DEMO-E-000015` (one annex) from the screen left the link with `3af08544…`, exactly the file's `sha256` in storage, and "Verify integrity" now says **1 / 1**. In groups, "Demostración" shows its 4 members and everything at level 3; the positive control with "Consulta" shows 1 member, permissions at 0 except its own at 1, and clearance **1 — PUBLICA**.
  900 archive-service tests + 188 integration tests against real PostgreSQL (16 new), 349 storage-service tests (4 new), 1,937 frontend tests (6 new), `svelte-check` 0/0, lint green across the 11 services. Mutated separately and red with their test: the resolver disconnected from the single link, the resolved value overriding the declared one in the batch, and the batch asking about items that already carried a hash.
  *Why*: an index that declares itself hashless while the files sit next to it with their SHA-256 does not meet RT-15, and whoever administers permissions cannot work without seeing the ones already granted — *Impact*: new `POST /storage/internal/files/hashes`, server-side hash resolution on link (single and batch), three read proxies under `/admin/grupos`, and the drawer showing the real state.

- `fix(storage,frontend)`: **the migration policy lived where the institution could not write it** (RF-PRE-04)
  The periodic sweep stated —in its docstring, in the config and in the documentation— that *"which format is obsolete is declared by the INSTITUTION, not by this code"*. And then it read **an environment variable**. An environment variable belongs to the DEPLOYMENT: the same for every institution sharing the installation, untouchable by the archivist, with no version, no author and no date. **The rule was written down and the code did the opposite.**
  The policy moves to `preservacion_plan.formatos_origen`, the other end of `formatos_destino` —present since the plan's first version— and it inherits the three properties that were missing: **append-only versioning** (changing the policy creates a new version; the previous one stays), **authorship** and **date**. Changing what gets converted in an institution's archive is an archival act and must be reviewable after the fact. It is declared from `/admin/preservacion`, where the archivist already administers the plan.
  **Precedence runs one way only: the institution's plan wins over the deployment value.** The variable remains an installation-wide default for institutions that have declared nothing — in a single-institution deployment the deployer **is** the institution; in a shared one each overrides it with its plan. The other way round, an institution could not change its own policy without asking whoever runs the containers.
  **And the "no policy" short-circuit can no longer be global.** Before, with the variable empty, the job returned **before looking at the database**: an institution that had declared its policy in its plan would have been ignored with nothing saying so. Now it asks institution by institution, and those declaring nothing are counted (`sin_politica`) instead of vanishing from the summary.
  **Two new ways to believe something was converted, closed.** A malformed MIME —`"msword"`, `"application/ msword"`— matches no file: the sweep would convert nothing while the policy read as declared on screen. The `type/subtype` shape is validated and rejected with `422` rather than stored, in the schema and in the SSR proxy too. And the comparison is now **case-insensitive** (RFC 2045 §5.1): the stored MIME is whatever the uploader sent, and an `Application/MSWord` fell outside a policy that does name it, yielding zero candidates with nothing appearing broken. It costs no index — there is none on `mime_type`.
  The column arrives with `DEFAULT '{}'`: a plan predating the migration declares nothing, and **nobody starts converting because they upgraded**. Verified against real PostgreSQL, which is where it shows.
  345 storage-service tests + 30 integration tests against real PostgreSQL (4 new), 19 frontend tests under `admin/preservacion` (2 new), `svelte-check` 0/0, lint green. Each guarantee mutated separately and red with its test: inverted precedence, `lower()` removed, MIME validation disabled, the column not reaching the `INSERT`, and the field not reaching the `PUT` from the screen.
  *Why*: a policy only writable by whoever runs the containers is not the institution's policy, however loudly the comment says otherwise — *Impact*: migration `storage tenant/016`, `formatos_origen` on `GET/PUT /api/v1/preservacion/plan`, a new field on `/admin/preservacion`, and `FORMAT_MIGRATION_MIME_TYPES` demoted to an installation-wide default.

- `fix(document)`: **the DIVIPOLA catalogue I loaded was not DANE's** (RF-RAD-05)
  The user asked hours after delivery: *"check whether DANE's DIVIPOLA changed"*. It had not merely changed — **what I loaded was never the official catalogue**.
  Migration `003` took it from the ICETEX Orfeo dump and verified it against **four known cases**: 05001 Medellín, 11001 Bogotá, 27150 Carmen del Darién, 91001 Leticia. All four were right. The rest was not checked. **Four hits say nothing about 1,135 rows**, and there was a downloadable official source I never even looked for.
  A mechanical comparison against DANE (Colombian Open Data, resource `gdxc-w37w`) found **38 differences**:
  **15 different names, and one was not a spelling variant but the WRONG municipality**: the dump carried `(muni_codi=21, dpto=05, 'PASTO')` — **Pasto attached to Antioquia**, under the code belonging to Alejandría. Pasto is 52001, in Nariño. Alejandría in turn appeared as 05076, which is not its code either. Verified in the dump itself: the source data is corrupt, it was not an extraction bug — it is the accumulated junk of a real installation, ironically the same kind of dirt this catalogue exists to prevent.
  **18 codes DIVIPOLA does not recognise**: Sincelejo under department 15 (Boyacá), Solano as 18765 instead of 18756, corregimientos and villages mixed in with municipalities.
  **5 missing municipalities**, including Tuchín (23815) and **Nuevo Belén de Bajirá** (27493), segregated from Riosucio and added by DANE in 2023.
  The 33 departments did match — they had been written by hand against the official denomination, which is exactly what was not done for the municipalities.
  **A new migration `004`; `003` is not rewritten.** The `public.schema_migrations` ledger identifies by `(service, file name)` and **stores no content hash**: editing `003` would leave any deployment that already applied it with the bad catalogue and nothing to warn about it. That is the silent failure this project already knows.
  A code DANE does not recognise is **not deleted**: it is flagged `vigente = false`. Deleting it would break a record already pointing at it, and the policy of not deleting a suppressed municipality was declared by `003` itself. It drops out of the cascade and still resolves a historical dispatch's name.
  **The missing guard COUNTS, which is what a single case does not do**: 1,122 current entities (1,103 municipalities + 18 non-municipalised areas + 1 island) and 33 departments, plus code↔department coherence across the whole catalogue. Mutated by leaving the catalogue uncorrected: both new tests go red.
  *Why*: a closed vocabulary holding the wrong municipality is worse than free text — free text at least does not claim to be official — *Impact*: migration `global/004`, 1,122 entities from DANE's source, and two tests that count instead of looking at four rows.

- `feat(document,storage,frontend)`: **geography is now chosen, not typed** (RF-RAD-05, DIVIPOLA cascade) **and format migration no longer needs to be invoked one file at a time** (RF-PRE-04, periodic sweep)
  The two items left declared as pending when their increments closed. They have nothing in common except the shape of the defect: **a capability that was built and had no door**.
  **1) The other half of the duplication problem.** RF-RAD-05's three defences tackled duplication of the SUBJECT. At the DESTINATION it stayed open: while `municipio` and `departamento` were free text, "Bogotá", "BOGOTA", "Bogota D.C." and "Santa Fe de Bogotá" were **four different municipalities** to every query, report and export. A uniqueness constraint does not close it — the four spellings ARE different and all of them pass; the only thing that closes it is the municipality no longer being something you type.
  The catalogue (33 departments, 1,135 municipalities) comes from the source Orfeo dump, which already carried the **correct DIVIPOLA codes and accented names** — verified against known cases: 05001 Medellín, 11001 Bogotá, 27150 Carmen del Darién, 91001 Leticia. It lives in `public` because it is national: the institution does not edit it, and replicating it per schema would copy data nobody can diverge. **2017 cut-off, and it is declared**: 94343 appears as "Guaviare" and is today Barrancominas, created in 2019. The load is idempotent so a newer dump can update it.
  **The asymmetry between the two tables is the substantive decision, and it is the frozen copy's lesson again.** In `tercero` the codes carry foreign keys to the catalogue — a live record must point at a municipality that exists; in `radicado_actor` they **do not**, on purpose: that row says where something was sent and has to survive the catalogue changing, including the municipality ceasing to exist. Tying it would turn a territorial reorganisation into a corruption of the historical record — or, with a `CASCADE`, would carry it away. Proven with a code that is NOT in the catalogue: with a foreign key, that insert would fail.
  **And the department is DERIVED from the municipality rather than believed from the client.** The municipality code contains its department's, so accepting the latter separately would mean accepting a contradiction you then have to detect. Deriving it makes it impossible, and a `CHECK (left(codigo,2) = departamento_codigo)` finishes the job in the database — this is the error a badly written cascade produces silently: the department changes and the previous department's municipality stays.
  **The stored name becomes the catalogue's official one**, not what was typed, so search, reports, the electronic index and export —which still read the text columns— stop seeing four spellings of the same municipality **without any of them changing**. The code is optional: an address abroad has no DIVIPOLA and existing records remain valid — requiring it would have closed the mail room over a presentation detail. For the same reason the municipality joined identity+destination deduplication: "Calle 10 #5-20" exists in half the country, and without it two legitimate dispatches to that address in two municipalities were rejected as duplicates.
  **One of my own tests passed with the mutation in place, and that was worth more than the fix.** The "changing department clears the previous municipality" case looked at the `<select>`: since emptying the options list already blanks the dropdown, it green-lit a cascade still sending `05001` with department `11`. Rewritten against the **emitted** value, which is what travels to the backend, and it now goes red. Fourth time a green test proved nothing.
  **2) Migration existed and could only be fired by hand, one file at a time.** An institutional archive holds hundreds of thousands of files. The sweep walks the active tenants and queues what is missing, with a per-sweep cap — converting is expensive and without one the first sweep would eat the machine.
  **What counts as obsolete is declared by the INSTITUTION, which is why the list ships empty.** That is an archival judgement depending on each institution's holdings and its PINAR; a job deciding it alone would be converting somebody's archive according to a list guessed by the vendor. With the policy undeclared the sweep walks the tenants and **touches not a single file**. Same criterion as the disposition sweep —counts and warns, never deletes— with one difference worth keeping in mind: **migrating destroys nothing**; the original is kept and the derivative lives alongside it. The danger is not converting too much, it is believing something was converted — which is why the sweep promotes nothing that did not pass the validator.
  **`fallido` and `no_verificado` are NOT the same, and conflating them has opposite consequences, none of them visible.** `fallido` speaks about the FILE —it could not be converted, or the result was non-conformant— and retrying it nightly costs a subprocess per file and fixes nothing. `no_verificado` speaks about the DEPLOYMENT —it converted and there was no validator to check it— so the day veraPDF is installed the next sweep resolves it on its own. Treating them alike either leaves the machine reconverting the impossible, or abandons forever files that were only waiting for a tool.
  430 document-service tests + 8 DIVIPOLA integration tests against real PostgreSQL, 364 storage-service tests + 6 queue integration tests, 14 frontend tests under `terceros/`, `svelte-check` 0/0, lint green across all 11 services. Mutated separately, each red under its own test: the membership `CHECK`, the absent foreign key on the frozen copy, the trigger over the code, clearing the municipality on department change, the guessed policy, and the `fallido`/`no_verificado` distinction.
  *Why*: a closed catalogue is the only thing that stops four spellings of one municipality from being four municipalities, and a conversion you must invoke file by file does not protect an archive of hundreds of thousands — *Impact*: two new catalogue endpoints, migrations `global/003` and `tenant/027`, the cascade in `/radicar`, and a sweep that is off by default with its policy in `FORMAT_MIGRATION_MIME_TYPES`.

- `fix(frontend,infra)`: **a browser poll was defeating the idle-session timeout**
  Keycloak closes the session after **30 minutes of inactivity** in production, and that control was correctly placed — on the server, not in the browser. What was not foreseen is that **the application itself would switch it off**.
  `ssoSessionIdleTimeout` resets every time the refresh token is used, and the transparent refresh in `hooks.server.js` fires on **any** request to the SvelteKit server — including the ones the app makes to itself. The mail inbox polls every 12 s while the tab is open. Result: **a tab left open on a desk kept the session alive up to the absolute 10-hour cap, with the screen unlocked.** Nobody decided that; it emerged from two individually reasonable things.
  **The fix is NOT to log out from the browser** — that would be a false control, bypassable by disabling JavaScript, and it would duplicate in the client a policy that already lives where it belongs. Instead the app **stops faking activity**: the poll halts after five minutes without real interaction, no refresh happens, and the server's timer does its job.
  **Hiding the tab does not count as activity; returning to it does.** The pause is announced on screen — an inbox that silently stops updating reads as an inbox with no news, which is the opposite of what is happening.
  *Impact*: the inbox poll pauses without real interaction; no change to the session policy, which was already correct.

- `fix(archive,frontend)`: **the trail recorded who downloaded each document and the screen never asked for it** (RF-BUS-10)
  The question was simple — "who accessed this document, who downloaded it?" — and the answer had been **stored all along**: `document.radicado_consultado` and `document.anexo_descargado`, written with actor, channel and security level.
  **What failed was the door.** The expediente's access history queried `object_type ∈ {expediente, expediente_indice}` by the **expediente code**, while those two traces use `object_type='radicado'` and `object_ref=<tracking number>`. The question had no answer on the screen built to answer it.
  The radicado actions admitted are an **admission list, reads only**, and the row now says **what it was about** — showing the reference and **not** the `payload`, which carries the document's classification level.
  **The tab's label dictionary matched NO real action** (`read`, `download`, `verify`…): everything fell through to `humanize`, and the "Download" entry made it look as though downloads were already shown. The map moves out of the `.svelte` into its own module — inside a template it could not be tested, which is why it drifted silently — with a test requiring every key to be a backend action name.
  **A stale comment denounced an already-closed hole**: the audit screen warned that `AuditQueryRepository` did not narrow by clearance. It does now (`_clearance_clause`, RF-SEG-08). The closure is written down rather than deleted — a warning about an open hole that is in fact plugged sends the next reader to audit what is already fine.
  *Impact*: the expediente's access history now includes reads and downloads of its documents.

- `feat(storage,frontend)`: **format migration against obsolescence — the system can now produce preservation copies** (RF-PRE-04)
  The last preservation requirement still in gap. PDF/A **validation** existed (real veraPDF since E10 C2b) and **migration** did not: the system could say a document was not PDF/A and do nothing about it.
  **THE ORIGINAL IS NEVER TOUCHED.** The migration produces a **derived** artifact alongside it — a system that replaces the original has lost the evidence the day someone disputes whether the conversion altered the content. The test proves it: the file repository is replaced by a double and the **only** call on the source must be the read.
  **FIXITY IS CHECKED BEFORE CONVERTING**, not after: migrating an already-corrupt file would propagate the corruption into the preservation copy and give it **the appearance of being the good one**. A mismatch answers `409` and records the attempt.
  **A fourth guarantee the rule does not ask for and practice does**: converting to PDF/A without checking the result IS PDF/A produces a copy nobody validated. Three outcomes, never conflated: `migrado`, `fallido` (bytes discarded) and `no_verificado` (converted but unverifiable — **not promoted** either).
  **THERE IS NO "STUB MIGRATOR", AND THAT IS THE DECISION.** The PDF/A validator's twin factory degrades to an honest stub because "not evaluated" is a real result. Here there is no equivalent: a stub would have to **produce a file**, and the only one it can produce without the tool is invented. The absence propagates as `None` up to a `503` that says what to install.
  The PREMIS `MIGRACION` event type had been in the vocabulary from the start with **nobody writing it**; now this does, with `hash_before` and `hash_after`.
  *Impact*: two new endpoints, migration `015`, `ghostscript` in the production image, and a "Migrate to PDF/A" action per annex. **RF-PRE-04 closed.**

- `feat(storage,archive,frontend)`: **the elimination record enters WORM — the debt phase 4 left declared** (E10 + RF-TRD-07)
  The record was left with **three local guarantees** —append-only trigger, SHA anchored in `audit_log`, XAdES signature— and **no Object-Lock**. The reason was declared then and was one of **model**: the WORM store assumed every protected artifact belongs to an **expediente**, and an elimination record covers N expedientes that are also about to disappear. Pinning it under one of their ids was not awkward: it was **wrong**.
  Migration `storage tenant/014` opens the model with a `CHECK` that **enforces the pairing**: `objeto_tipo` (`expediente` | `lote`) says what `expediente_id` refers to, and the constraint closes both impossible combinations. Without it a row could claim an electronic index belongs to a batch, and the purge gate —which queries by expediente— would stop seeing it **with nothing to warn**.
  `expediente_id` is **not renamed**, and the cost is declared: renaming would touch 142 references across ten modules, most of which are about actual expedientes. What does change is that the purge gate and the per-expediente listing now require `objeto_tipo = 'expediente'` **explicitly** — the two id spaces not colliding "in practice" is not a control.
  The record joins `CONTROL_INSTRUMENT_TIPOS`: like the transfer record it outlives the content and **must not postpone its disposition** — and all the more so, since its retention is permanent and treating it as content would make it **the instrument blocking the very act it documents**.
  **A repo guard had a blind spot, and this change was its first exposure**: `test_preservacion_tipos_guard.py` forces every new `tipo` in the `CHECK` to be classified, but read migration 010 **by name**. Migration 014 widens the `CHECK` and the guard would have kept reading 010, accepting an incomplete taxonomy **without failing**. *A guard pointing at a fixed file expires the day someone writes the next one.*
  **Permanent preservation = 100 years**, the contract's maximum, because Object-Lock demands a **date**, not an infinity. Renewable through the same path as Total Conservation series.
  *Impact*: `objeto_tipo` in the WORM store (backwards compatible: existing rows become `expediente`), two migrations, and the lock's state visible on screen. E04 has no loose ends left.

- `feat(frontend)`: **PINAR stops being a dashboard that can never be filled** (RF-ADM-08)
  The screen consumed **6 of the module's 31 endpoints**: a plan could be created and walked through its lifecycle, but there was no way to create a critical aspect, an objective or a project from the browser. The dashboard was always empty and the page itself called it "a later PINAR phase". A dashboard that can never be filled is not an empty state: it is a **false green**.
  Three new tabs. **The screen mirrors the service's two lifecycles, which never overlap**: structure is editable only in `borrador`, follow-up only when `aprobado`/`en_ejecucion`. And the gate is **two** conditions, not one: the plan's state **and** `USUA_PERM_ADMIN` — the permission gate was missing in the first version and **its own test caught it**.
  **The backend computes the priority**; the table is shown already sorted by it, and "not prioritised" is **not** painted as priority zero. **A project's progress is never typed in**: it comes from its append-only follow-up cut-offs, which is why `avance_pct` is not in the create/edit allow-list.
  **A proxy factory instead of eleven files**: the allow-list is the anti mass-assignment barrier, and eleven copies of a barrier drift apart silently. *Impact*: 2 → 5 tabs, 6 → 31 endpoints consumed, no backend changes.

- `feat(archive,document,storage,knowledge,frontend)`: **secure deletion — the retention schedule finally destroys what it orders destroyed** (RF-TRD-07 phase 5 of 5: epic closed)
  The system **preserved well and never destroyed**. Constitution invariant 12 held without a crack, and that was simultaneously the non-compliance: keeping everything forever is not applying the retention schedule, it is **not** applying it (Law 594/2000 art. 24; AGN Agreement 001/2024 art. 4.3.2.5).
  **It takes no body**, the most deliberate design decision of the phase: in a destruction there is nothing for the client to parametrise. On screen the button requires **typing the batch code**.
  **Order is half the design**: bytes before rows (otherwise orphan objects in MinIO nobody can attribute — impossible to find and therefore to delete); documents before the expediente (otherwise the radicado links cascade away and **there is no way left to know what had to be destroyed**); the headstone before the deletion and in its own transaction (otherwise a destroyed expediente with no headstone, and silence is indistinguishable from "never existed").
  **All-or-nothing per expediente**, and `purge_for_disposition` **had had no door since it was written**. **Migration 049 released three foreign keys and one averted a silent loss**: `transferencias` was `ON DELETE CASCADE` and would have taken `transferencia_acta` with it — a control instrument that storage-service's own code declares must outlive the content.
  *Impact*: RF-TRD-07 closed, and with it E04 complete.

- `feat(archive,signature,frontend)`: **the elimination record — what outlives what was eliminated** (RF-TRD-07 phase 4)
  Permanent preservation, append-only by trigger, canonical XML with its SHA-256 anchored in `audit_log`, XAdES signature. **The record's inventory is complete, never clearance-narrowed** — what is narrowed is *who reads it*, with the threshold derived from the highest level in the inventory itself. `acta_eliminacion` had to enter signature-service's XAdES allow-list: without it the record would have fallen back to the `nativa` HMAC **with no error and no visible difference**.

- `feat(archive,frontend)`: **the Committee's endorsement, batch withdrawal, and the page that was missing** (RF-TRD-07 phase 3)
  **No endorsement while the publicity period is still open**, guarded in the transition's own `WHERE` with the server clock. The `409` distinguishes all three reasons. And the phase-2 chokepoint was leaving a case out silently: a published-then-withdrawn batch vanished from the public view. Rewritten as the **mark of the act** (`publicado_at IS NOT NULL`).


- `feat(archive,frontend)`: **prior publicity — the inventory of what is to be destroyed, in plain sight** (RF-TRD-07, phase 2 of 5)
  A published batch stops being an internal matter: its inventory becomes visible **without authentication**, with the deadline to object, linked from the portal. Without prior publicity, the Committee endorses something nobody outside could look at.
  **The objection channel is not invented**: it is the institution's existing **single window**. A separate objection form would be a second inbox nobody watches.
  **The chokepoint is the batch's STATE, not a deployment switch** — deliberately not tied to `public_interop_enabled`: publishing the correspondence register is the institution's *decision*; publishing what it is about to destroy is a *duty*. A chokepoint is reused when the policy is the same, not because the name fits.
  **A classified expediente's description is not published, but its row is**, and the narrowing lives in the `SELECT`. Omitting the row would fail the duty precisely over what matters most to object to; what is withheld is the **denomination**. The page **says so** rather than leaving a mute gap.
  Publishing **freezes the inventory**, and `publicado_hasta` is mandatory and future, **with no default**.
  **A test of mine did not discriminate, and mutation exposed it.** The unit test mocked `fetchrow` returning `None`, so mutating the `WHERE ... AND estado = 'borrador'` left it **green**: the "0 rows" came from the mock. Fixed on both sides: the unit test now asserts on the **emitted SQL**, and the real race is proven in the **integration lane** against real PostgreSQL.
  **Another non-existent colour token**: `--op-text-muted` (it is `--op-text-secondary`). Fourth appearance of the same silent failure.
  832 archive-service unit tests + 6 integration, 1837 frontend, svelte-check 0/0, lint green.
  *Why*: destroying a document without anyone outside being able to look at the inventory is not a procedure — *Impact*: new public page. **Still nothing is deleted.**

- `feat(archive,api-gateway,frontend)`: **the elimination batch — first of the five phases of the only remaining requirement** (RF-TRD-07)
  Elimination happens **by batch** because the record of proceedings and the Committee's endorsement belong to the batch. This phase proposes and **freezes the inventory**; publishing, endorsing, signing the record and executing secure deletion are the four that follow, and the last one **is not built**. `ejecutado` always travels as `false`.
  **A resource of its own, not an appendix of `/trd`**: a batch is a procedure with a lifecycle, and each transition has constancies enforced by `CHECK` — publishing without a deadline gives no time to object, and endorsing without a record, date and administrative act is an unverified assertion.
  **The inventory is frozen, and not as a convenience copy**: it is what gets published for third parties to object to, and the expediente is going to cease to exist. A trigger enforces it: **never editable**, and removable **only while the batch is a draft**.
  **Five admission conditions**: a confirmed due date (without a decision there is no proposal — the only barrier against automatic destruction); a disposition that **ends in destruction** (`E`, `S`, `M`; Conservación Total **never** enters); a frozen instrument that governs it; an **expired WORM lock**; and not already being in a live batch.
  **There are THREE locks, and the longest one wins.** An expediente may carry `worm_retain_until` on its signed index, its transfer record and its AIP; while one stands, COMPLIANCE mode lets **nobody** delete, not even the administrator. Taking the first one found would yield a batch that blows up on execution, with the Committee already convened.
  **Rejections are reported, not dropped.** Whoever proposes 40 and gets 31 must see which nine are missing and why. An expediente outside the proposer's clearance is reported as "not found", **indistinguishable from a non-existent one**.
  **With its door on the same day**: only rows already confirmed whose disposition destroys carry a checkbox, and the `409` with its reasons is forwarded as-is instead of collapsing into "could not create batch".
  Migration `archive tenant/047` validated against real PostgreSQL. 823 archive-service tests, 1828 frontend, svelte-check 0/0, lint green. **The five conditions verified by mutation one by one.**
  *Why*: preserving everything forever is not complying with the TRD, it is failing to apply it — *Impact*: `/api/v1/eliminacion/` joins the gateway. **Still nothing is deleted.**

- `feat(archive)`: **the sweep that runs on its own, and the alert that reaches whoever is not looking** (RF-TRD-08 + R.4.8)
  Fourth step of the increment that **destroys nothing**, closing the scheduled trigger. The due-disposition queue had to be visited; now the system sweeps by itself and **alerts whoever can decide**.
  **No new infrastructure was needed**: `archive-service` already had two background loops with exactly the right shape — a testable `run_once`, their own kill switch and interval, never dying on an exception. This is a third with the same mould, and its interval **is** the alert cadence, so there is no per-tenant "last sent" state to keep in sync. **What silences the alert is deciding, not having read it.**
  **The plan's recommendation was false and was corrected before building on it.** It said "start in-app", assuming E16 had a notifications channel. **It does not**: the frontend store is ephemeral session toasts and `notification-service` is email only. A toast is not an alert — it vanishes in four seconds and is seen only by someone already looking, precisely who the alert is not for.
  **The alert is composed PER RECIPIENT**, and there lay the trap the plan flagged in advance: the count is narrowed by the recipient's clearance. Telling someone with clearance for 9 that they have "14 pending" tells them **5 classified expedientes exist**. Verified against real PostgreSQL: a classified overdue expediente counts for clearance 3 and **not** for 1 or 2. Whoever has nothing due **gets no email**.
  **Recipients are resolved by RBAC query**, with the same criterion as the deciding endpoint's gate, never a configured mailing list: a list drifts from the permissions silently.
  **The email is deliberately poor**: a number, a deadline and where to go. No tracking numbers, no subjects, no series — email leaves the institution and lands in mailboxes the SGDEA does not govern. It also states that **coming due is not deleting**.
  Failures isolated per tenant **and per recipient**. A form guardian verifies the job **executes no disposition**. 808 archive-service tests, lint green, migrations `045`/`046` applied and verified on the demo tenant. Three mutations verified.
  *Why*: a queue you must remember to visit does not satisfy "alert authorized users" — *Impact*: a third background loop that sends email; it requires `NOTIFICATION_INTERNAL_TOKEN` and refuses to alert without it. **Still nothing is deleted.**

- `feat(archive,frontend)`: **the authorized user's confirmation — "no destruction is automatic"** (RF-TRD-06)
  Third step of the increment that **destroys nothing**, and the one that turns the due-disposition queue into more than information: an authorized user now **decides**. It is rule 4 of the TRD requirements — "no destruction is automatic without an authorized user's confirmation" — and until today it existed nowhere.
  **Confirming still deletes nothing, and the response says so.** `ejecutada: false` travels explicitly with a note, because a `201` on an endpoint called "decide" invites the belief that something happened; the screen's modal repeats it before the person clicks.
  **The ADR-025 D5 gate finally gets a caller.** `assert_disposicion_final_autorizada` had been written, tested and **called by nobody** — its own docstring said so. It now blocks confirming a deletion whose frozen instrument does not govern it, which is the right moment: rejecting it at the end of the procedure, once the Committee has been convened, would be discovering the limit by failing. **`CT` is exempt**; `E`, `S` and `M` are not.
  **What is not accepted from the client is the point.** The due date and disposition code do **not** travel in the body — the backend resolves them from the frozen snapshot; otherwise confirming a deletion would be a matter of sending the right date. Out-of-reach expedientes answer **404, not 403**.
  **Deferring is a decision, not hiding something** (Model SGDEA R.4.9): it requires a reason of at least 10 characters and a **future** return date, validated in the proxy, the schema and a migration `CHECK`. The item leaves the active queue and **comes back on its own**.
  Decisions live in `disposicion_decisiones` (migration `archive tenant/046`), **append-only**, storing the **frozen** due date they were made about — a 2026 confirmation cannot authorize a deletion a reformulated TRD orders in 2030. The immutable audit entry is written **in the same transaction**.
  Migration validated against real PostgreSQL. 801 archive-service tests, 1824 frontend, svelte-check 0/0, lint green. Five mutations verified separately.
  *Why*: a calendar that comes due with nobody deciding is an archive administering itself, and the rule requires the opposite — *Impact*: a new endpoint and two actions in the fourth tab of `/admin/trd`. **Still nothing is deleted.**

- `feat(archive,frontend)`: **the system stored the retention calendar and never said it out loud** — the due-disposition queue (RF-TRD-08)
  Second step of the increment that **destroys nothing**. On closing an expediente the system freezes its calendar — `fin_archivo_gestion`, `fin_archivo_central`, the disposition code and the act that validated the series — and there it stayed: correct data nobody ever read. `GET /api/v1/trd/disposicion-vencida` surfaces it, and a fourth tab in `/admin/trd` puts it in front of whoever decides. **An endpoint with no door is the pattern this review has been chasing all along**, so it ships with its own on day one.
  **Coming due is not an order to destroy, and the UI cannot let it read that way.** Under Conservación Total, the central archive coming due means transfer to the historical archive and preserve **forever**; showing that row beside the deletion ones undifferentiated would turn the queue into a "list of things to delete" containing precisely the expedientes that are never deleted. Every row therefore carries its due action, **derived from the frozen snapshot** and written by admission.
  **`gobierna_eliminacion` does not reimplement the gate.** The ADR-025 D5 criterion was extracted into a single predicate now shared by the `assert` that blocks the act and the queue that merely reports. Copying the condition into the query would have been reimplementing the thing under verification, and the divergence would surface the day someone touched one side.
  **The delicate part is the count, not the page.** No-read-up sits in the **same** condition as the rows, `COUNT` included: narrowing only the page would let the total betray how many classified expedientes are due. The query also leaves an aggregate trace (RF-BUS-10).
  **A non-existent colour token caught before it shipped**: the notice used `var(--op-danger)`, which does not exist (it is `--op-error`). The browser would have dropped the whole declaration **silently**.
  787 archive-service tests, 1811 frontend, svelte-check 0/0, lint green. Four mutations verified.
  *Why*: a calendar that lives only in the database governs nothing, because nobody looks at it — *Impact*: the institution sees for the first time what came due, when, under which act and what is due. **No act is executed**: effective deletion (RF-TRD-07) remains unbuilt.

- `fix(archive)`: **an expediente closed without a TRD series left the lifecycle silently**
  First increment of the largest remaining product gap: **final disposition is never executed**. The system has the calendar — on closing it freezes `fin_archivo_gestion`, `fin_archivo_central`, the disposition code and the act that validated the series into `expedientes.disposition` — and it has the authorizing gate (`assert_disposicion_final_autorizada`, ADR-025 D5, **written and with no caller**). What it lacks is the executing arm. This increment is deliberately **the one that destroys nothing**.
  **And looking at it surfaced an older hole.** Closing an expediente **without a TRD series** left `disposition = NULL` and `trd_revision_requerida = false`: no retention calendar, no disposition date and **absent from every queue**. That is not an over-preserved expediente — it is one that leaves the archival lifecycle silently, because nothing names it and nobody will look at it again. Confirmed on the demo tenant: **1 expediente in that state**, invisible to `/trd/revision-pendiente`.
  The `cierre_sin_snapshot_de_retencion` reason **had existed since migration 037**, which used it to mark historical closures; the third tab of `/admin/trd` knew how to present it; and **no code path ever wrote it afterwards**. The backfill repaired the past and **nothing protected the future** — precisely the hole `audit_log`'s three defences had, covering modification and deletion with none covering insertion.
  **The fix is two halves, and the first alone would have been inert.** The service now marks the calendar-less closure; but the revision marks lived inside `if pin_trd:`, true only when the expediente **has** a series — exactly the opposite case. They are now written **on close**; on **transfer** they are still left alone, because the method's defaults would erase a pending mark nobody has resolved. Both halves verified by mutation **separately**.
  It **marks, it does not block**, the same criterion this method already applies to `migrada` series (ADR-025 D6). Migration `archive tenant/045` sweeps what slipped through between 037 and today, idempotent and validated against real PostgreSQL. 776 archive-service tests, lint green.
  *Why*: an expediente with no retention calendar is not preserved, it is unwatched — *Impact*: closures without a TRD series now surface in the third tab of `/admin/trd`, and the migration collects the earlier ones.

- `fix(archive,tenant,frontend)`: **the system was hiding the PINAR and the TRD while serving them**
  Closes the product decision that the negative sweep (B13) left open in `docs/es/plan-pruebas.md` §4.bis rather than settling on its own. The question: TRD and PINAR reads required no permission at all, while the sidebar hid the whole Administration section and `/admin` gated its cards. Gate them, or declare them open — both were legitimate — but **what was there was not defensible**.
  **The first thing that changed was the size of the problem.** The §4.bis count — "TRD 4, PINAR 11" — was written by hand. A census BY FORM over both services' routers found **33 ungated reads** reachable through the gateway: TRD's 4, **4 TVD ones the question never mentioned**, PINAR's 11, and 14 catalog reads.
  **With the full list the dilemma dissolved**, because those reads are not one thing. The **classification vocabulary** — TRD series and TVD groupings (list and detail), retention, document types, dependencias, catalogs, parameters — **stays open to any authenticated user of the tenant, now declared in code and covered by a test**: `/radicar` and `/expedientes` consume it in order to classify, and `USUA_PERM_TRD` belongs to whoever **administers** the instrument, not to whoever uses it. The validated instrument must be published anyway (Ac. AGN 004/2019, compiled by 001/2024; Ley 1712/2014 art. 9).
  The **circuit** is gated with `USUA_PERM_TRD`: `GET /trd/{code}/versiones` and its TVD twin return the full history — drafts, `acta_comite`, `fecha_aprobacion_comite`, `instancia_convalidante` and `created_by` of every version — the Committee's deliberation, not the published instrument. Its twin `GET /revision-pendiente` already required it.
  **PINAR's 11 reads now require `USUA_PERM_ADMIN`**, gated on the `APIRouter` itself — by admission, so tomorrow's endpoint inherits it — and **deleting the docstring sentence** that declared the opposite: a stale comment is the vector, not decoration. It closes rather than opens because a plan in `borrador` is the Committee's working material, and because publishing the approved PINAR (Decreto 1080/2015 art. 2.8.2.5.8) is an act directed outwards — a public screen — not an ungated internal GET that resembles one.
  On the frontend, `/admin/pinar`'s `load` put the `403` in the same bucket as network failures; it now travels as `sinPermiso`, the screen names the missing permission, and it **obeys the backend even when the session disagrees**.
  773 archive-service tests, 117 tenant-service, 1804 frontend; all five fixes verified by mutation.
  *Why*: a system that hides something and serves it at the same time has decided nothing — *Impact*: **PINAR's 11 reads and the two TRD/TVD version histories now answer 403 without the permission**; the classification vocabulary is unchanged and `/radicar` behaves as before.

- `fix(frontend)`: **an unreachable inbox announced its list might be out of date**
  Found **in the browser**, not in a test, while validating the freshly added scope gate: `jefe` (an administrator) opened another officer's **personal** mailbox inbox by direct URL. The backend refused with the neutral `404` — correct, and the screen explained it well — but it **also** showed, in amber, "we cannot check for news, so this list may be out of date; refresh it by hand before registering". Over an inbox that does not exist, with no list and nothing to register. The poller kept asking every 12s and its `404` read as a transient failure: **an authorisation error presented as something else**, the same defect that had `/envios` present a `403` as "no records".
  With the inbox unreachable there is no polling, the poller-down notice stays quiet, and "Consultar el buzón" no longer offers an impossible act. All three consequences hang off a single condition, with a test that the condition does not vanish; verified by mutation and checked live: no polling calls on the other person's account, and polling **present** on one's own.
  *Why*: a message blaming a network failure for what is a refusal of access sends you looking for the fault where it is not — *Impact*: whoever opens an inbox that is not theirs reads one explanation, and it is the true one.

- `fix(document,frontend)`: **the mailbox inbox gate said who may register, not which mailbox**
  A security finding, surfaced while documenting the inbox endpoints and discovering they were not documented at all.
  **`PERM_RADI` authorises working with email; which mailbox is decided by a different cut** — the `WHERE` in `email_cuenta_repository`, carefully written and tested, which hides an officer's **personal** account even from the administrator. Listing the inbox, reserving, releasing and discarding went straight to the message by id, **bypassing it**: anyone with `PERM_RADI` in the institution could read the subject and sender of another officer's private mail and set it aside. Only `sincronizar` and `radicar` crossed the cut, because they needed it to open the IMAP session — that is, the gate reached where it was technically required, not where it was required for safety. This is the second time in this repository that a correct cut fails to reach the door next to it.
  It is closed with a **single** guard in the service — the check repeated at every endpoint is the one that will one day be missing — answering `404 cuenta_no_encontrada`, not `403`: telling them apart would turn the endpoint into an oracle of which mailboxes the institution has and who works them. Plus a second layer in the mutation's `WHERE`: `cuenta_id` binds the message to the mailbox, because otherwise reaching **one** would be enough to operate on any other's mail knowing only its id. Both layers verified by mutation separately.
  **Discarding a message now writes to the immutable chain**, in the same transaction and **not** best-effort: if `audit_log` cannot take it, the message is not discarded. Deciding that a received communication is **not** registered is as much a decision as registering it — it is the one that makes the document disappear from the archive — and until now it left only an `UPDATE` on an ordinary table, rewritable afterwards without a trace. The entry carries neither subject nor sender: a discarded message was never registered, so it has no classification level with which to cut its readability, and in `audit_log` every `USUA_PERM_ADMIN` would see it. The reason does travel, because it is the text of the decision, not of the document.
  **And yet another capability built and never offered**: `liberar()` had existed in the repository with its test since Increment 2 with no way to call it. The effect was that opening a message, seeing it was not yours and closing the screen left it locked **for fifteen minutes for the whole front desk**, with no way out but waiting. There is now an endpoint, closing the form releases the reservation, and your own row offers "Soltarlo".
  document-service's integration lane gains the `public.audit_log` bootstrap ported from archive-service: the entry's atomicity cannot be checked with a double, because the rollback is produced by Postgres. And `docs/{es,en}/api.md` finally document all **seven** inbox endpoints, cross-checked against the running service's OpenAPI — not read.
  466 document-service tests (14 integration ones over the inbox), lint green.
  *Why*: a permission that says "may register" does not say "may use this mailbox", and an officer's personal mail does not belong to the front desk — *Impact*: anyone without an assigned mailbox no longer reaches its inbox; personal accounts are visible only to their owner, from the inbox too.

- `refactor(document,frontend)`: **the single-mailbox legacy route (`IMAP_*`) is retired**
  Closes E19 Increment 3. Gone: the `IMAP_HOST`, `IMAP_PORT`, `IMAP_USER`, `IMAP_PASSWORD`, `IMAP_USE_SSL`, `IMAP_MAILBOX` and `IMAP_TENANT_SLUG` variables, the `GET /api/v1/ingest/email/estado` and `POST /api/v1/ingest/email/poll` endpoints, the `imaplib` client and ingestor behind them, and the "legacy mailbox" block of `/admin/correo` with its history table.
  **Not cosmetic cleanup: that route could not do the job.** One mailbox per deployment does not express what Acuerdo AGN 060/2001 art. 13 requires; the password lived in clear text in a container's environment; and above all it **registered every unread message in one go** — advertising and newsletters included — because it had nowhere to separate what gets registered from what does not. It is replaced by what the three increments built: one row per mailbox with its encrypted credential, its filtering folder, its "Radicados" folder, its mode and its responsible officer.
  **A contradiction disappears at the root, too.** The screen used to say "email ingestion is not configured" with a configured, working mailbox right below it: two sources of truth talking at once. That was patched by conditioning the notice; now the second source does not exist, which is the strong fix. The guardian that watched the condition now watches that.
  The record of what was registered from email **is not lost with the history table**: every radicado leaves its entry in the immutable audit trail, and each account's inbox keeps which message produced which number — and which mailbox it came from, something the poll history never distinguished.
  409 document-service tests (the 15 of the retired route go with it), 9 for `/admin/correo`, `svelte-check` 0/0, lint green.
  *Why*: keeping two ingestion routes forces a decision about which one wins, and the legacy one registered the advertising — *Impact*: **anyone with `IMAP_*` in their environment must configure their mailbox from `/admin/correo`**; the variables are ignored and both endpoints return 404.

- `fix(document,frontend)`: **an incoming document could be annulled with the permission alone — and that act does not exist for incoming documents**
  A domain correction from the user, opening an **axis this work had not been auditing**. Every permission review of these days answers "is this person authorised?". There is a prior question: "is this act possible on this kind of document?".
  **A radicado de Entrada is not annulled.** If a document entered the institution, it entered: that is a fact, not a revisable decision. Annulment exists for what the institution **produces** — a Salida or Interno issued by mistake. The service only checked existence and prior annulment, so `PERM_SOL_ANULAR` was enough to annul an Entrada. Legacy Orfeo did distinguish (`sgd_trad_codigo`).
  The rule lives in `app/core/reglas_tipo.py`, written by **admission** (declare which types admit each act, never which are excluded — with an exclusion list the omission lands on the permissive side and says nothing). **Not even ROOT bypasses it**: ROOT's bypass is over authorisation, not over what is possible. `409`, not `403`: the caller is not missing a permission — granting one would change nothing.
  Both halves fixed: the inbox offered "Solicitar anulación" looking only at the permission, so it painted the button on an Entrada.
  *Impact*: `409 acto_no_aplica_al_tipo` on an Entrada, with or without permission, with or without ROOT. Verified by mutation in both layers.
  **Open**: this is the first rule of its kind to be declared. If there are more per-type particularities, `reglas_tipo.py` is now where they belong — and enumerating them at once beats discovering them one at a time.

- `fix(document,auth)`: **the permission catalogue promised nine controls and enforced one**
  The question was what to do with `PERM_RADI_ENTRADA` / `PERM_RADI_INTERNO`. The inverse sweep — *what is seeded that nobody requires*, the reverse of the one that caught the six phantom permissions — found that of **26 seeded permissions, nine** are required by no gate and no row. `USUA_PERM_ROOT` is legitimate (it is the `is_root` bypass). **`SGD_PERM_ESTADISTICA` was a real hole**: the UI honours it (the "Reportes" menu and the dashboard metrics) and the backend did not require it, so anyone authenticated could call the reports endpoints directly — the third appearance today of a control living only in the browser. Now gated, with the denial verified to happen **before** touching the database. The remaining seven have no reference anywhere in the repository.
  On the original question: the per-type trio **cannot be enforced today** because `PERM_RADI_SALIDA` is already taken — it gates the six postal-dispatch endpoints, not registering Salidas. Enforcing only two of three would be a half-control that looks whole; enforcing the third would either lock out everyone who registers Salidas or, backfilled, **widen** postal-dispatch access to everyone who can register — a security regression. Rewiring that name first is a product decision, not a fix.
  Descriptions are corrected rather than rows deleted: the FK is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so deleting would silently drop grants an institution already made. A new guardian keeps the declared list of inert permissions honest, failing in **both** directions.
  *Impact*: reports require `SGD_PERM_ESTADISTICA`; migrations `auth-service/018` (descriptions only, including `PERM_RADI_SALIDA`, whose name misleads about what it gates).

- `fix(document,storage)`: **reclassifying with read permission, uploading files with no permission at all, and registering a draft nobody approved**
  Closing the debt the MCP plan kept declaring without fixing. All six findings were verified against the code before being touched, and **two dissolved on inspection** — said anyway, because a false finding left in a report wastes as much time as a real one left unreported.
  `PATCH /documents/{id}/security-level` required `PERM_RECLASIFICAR` at the default `min_crud` (1 = read), so holding it for consultation alone was enough to raise or lower a record's classification. Both its twins already required 3 and said why. `POST /storage/upload` — the only endpoint of storage-service the gateway exposes — had **no gate at all**; it now requires `PERM_RADI` at level 1 (taken from its only real consumer, the radicación flow; nobody currently working loses access). `radicar_borrador` did not require the draft to be approved, although the UI does: a control living only in the browser is not a control (D-05).
  `PERM_RADI_ENTRADA` / `PERM_RADI_INTERNO` still restrict nothing (0 gates). Enforcing them is a product decision that collides with `PERM_RADI_SALIDA` currently meaning "manage postal dispatch"; retiring them would drop existing grants. Migration `017` fixes what is unacceptable meanwhile: the **description** an administrator reads before granting.
  Two of my own findings were false: `/vistos-buenos/decidir` authorises by identity (chain membership + turn), which is stronger than a generic permission; and the `min_crud` on expedientes is a coherent convention (3 = irreversible), not an oversight.
  *Impact*: reclassification now needs write level; uploads need `PERM_RADI`; drafts must be approved before registering. Migration `auth-service/017` (descriptions only).

- `fix(auth,mcp)`: **six workflow permissions the code required that nobody could hold — and the guardian meant to catch them had been skipping all along**
  Found by the plan's **end-to-end verification** with the stack up and a real tenant: `auth_permissions` held 20 rows and the code required six that were absent (`PERM_TRAMITAR`, `PERM_VOBO`, `PERM_ANULAR`, `PERM_CERRAR_EXP`, `PERM_INFORMAR`, `PERM_AGENDAR`). A permission not in that table cannot be granted to any group, so the gate denies everyone but ROOT: the twelve seeded transaction types and the five workflow writes were ROOT-only. Fail-closed — the right side — and still capability that exists in code and no institution could distribute.
  It is exactly the `RADI_CREAR` defect that opened this plan, at larger scale. **And the guardian written to prevent it was switched off**: it lived in the mcp-server image, which does not mount the repository, so it could not find the migrations and called `pytest.skip` on every run — "11 passed, 1 skipped" reads just like "12 passed". Moved to `shared/tests/` (the only CI lane that sees the whole repo, where skipping is impossible) and widened to all three sources of requirement: the MCP catalogue, every router's `require_permission`, and the `requires_permission` column of seeded rows. The six missing ones are declared by none of them but the last two — looking at one source is how they survived a control bearing their name.
  *Impact*: additive migration `auth-service/016_permissions_tramite.sql`; **run the auth-service runner on every existing tenant** or workflows stay ROOT-only. mcp-server goes from "142 passed, 1 skipped" to **143 passed, 0 skipped**.

- `test(e2e)`: **the plan's success criterion, verified against a running stack**
  Gateway routes `/api/v1/mcp/` (200 vs `route_not_found` on controls); the scope framework returns 15 executable / 29 readable / 1 denied with its reason from real permissions; invoking a write returns a signed proposal and the database does not change; confirming without the phrase gives `confirmacion_requerida` and it still does not change; with the phrase, 201 and a real tracking number; replaying the token gives `propuesta_consumida`; the record carries the real user's `registered_by` and its `audit_log` entry with the correct actor. Negative control: a tool whose permission the user lacks is absent from the catalogue and returns 403 without touching the database.
  *Declared pending*: the assistant turn with the real model needs `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and was not exercised; everything deterministic — where the gates live — was.

- `feat(frontend)`: **voice dictation — and the button does not appear if dictating would send the voice to a third party**
  `VoiceInput.svelte` in the assistant composer: `es-CO` recognition, interim results painted over a base so the phrase does not repeat word by word, and errors that say what they are (permission, microphone, language).
  **The plan's premise was false, so the design changed.** It justified browser recognition with "the audio never leaves the device" and claimed this served sovereignty *better* than ADR-020's Whisper `transcribe` endpoint. In Chrome and Edge the classic Web Speech API **sends the audio to a Google recognition service**. ADR-020 had decided the opposite in writing. Shipping it as planned would have sent a public servant's voice to a third party, in a system holding reserved records, under a written justification asserting the reverse.
  So the button is not offered because the API exists: only when the browser can recognise **on device** and says so itself (`availableOnDevice` for that language, plus `processLocally`). A `downloadable` model does not count. The check is fail-closed by construction — no availability API, or a throw, means no button. Worst case is no dictation; never dictation leaving the device unnoticed.
  Dictation never sends nor confirms: it fills the composer and stops there. A write proposal is approved by hand, on its card.
  ADR-020 is annotated with what was built and its limit: the Whisper `transcribe` endpoint remains **unbuilt** and is the only sovereign path for every browser.
  *Why*: last item of the plan, and its written justification did not hold — *Impact*: dictation where the browser recognises locally, invisible where it does not; 1488 vitest tests, `svelte-check` 0/0.

- `feat(mcp,frontend)`: **the assistant promised a confirmation in writing that did not exist — the card is now built**
  The assistant panel stated that "every write action will ask for explicit confirmation" over an interface **that was never built**: no proposal rendering, no dialog, no buttons. The backend solved it from the other side by forbidding all writes (v1 read-only). Both halves were consistent with each other and neither delivered the promise.
  Backend: the assistant no longer excludes write tools from what the model sees. A `tool_use` on one **never reaches the dispatcher**: it becomes a signed proposal carried in `AssistantMessageResponse.propuestas` (additive field, no coordinated deploy). The `tool_result` returned to the model states `ejecutado: false` and "NOTHING HAS BEEN EXECUTED… do not chain actions that depend on its result" — without that anchor the model reads its own `tool_use` as having taken effect and writes "I registered the document", a false claim about an official record. The proposal token is never put in the model's context.
  Irreversible acts require a typed phrase, **enforced by the backend from the signed token** — the client cannot claim an act was not irreversible, because it is not the client who says so. The UI disables the button as a courtesy; the barrier lives in `write_gate`, since a phrase validated only in the browser is theatre (D-05). In-band elicitation asks for the phrase too, so irreversible acts are not the only ones that path cannot approve.
  Frontend: `ProposalCard.svelte` in the dock, an `/asistente/api/confirmar` proxy, per-proposal state. One confirmation **per act**, chains included. The card is written by the catalogue and its values are rendered as text, never HTML — the model composed them from a conversation that may include an incoming document's body, i.e. a third party's text.
  Three of my own defects, each caught by its test: a `ProposalError` escaping and collapsing the whole turn with a 500; the confirm proxy's allowlist stripping the very phrase it needed to forward; and a CRUD-level test that survived its mutation because it checked the first barrier twice and the second one never.
  *Why*: a promise of confirmation with no interface behind it is worse than no promise — the person trusts a control that does not exist — *Impact*: the assistant can prepare registrations, expedientes, links, assignments and drafts; none executes until someone approves it on its card.

- `fix(mcp,gateway)`: **the permissions header nobody produced, the env setting nobody consulted, and a scope framework only the web assistant could see**
  Three places read `X-User-Permissions`, a header no component produces — the gateway drops it as forgeable — so it always arrived empty: `GET /api/v1/mcp/tools`, the endpoint that exists to tell someone what they can do, answered everyone with the permission-free tools. `X-User-Roles` **does** arrive, so ROOT saw everything and the defect stayed invisible to exactly the person who could spot it. Its stdio twin was worse: `MCP_USER_PERMISSIONS`/`MCP_USER_ROLES` *could* be set, so whoever configured them believed they were granting something, when all they got was tools offered that the backend would deny. Both settings are removed; identity comes from `GET /auth/me` on all three transports, `permission_levels` included (`build_tools` did not pass `levels`, so read-level `PERM_RADI` showed `tipificar_radicado`, which requires create level).
  The scope framework reaches MCP: `mi_alcance` tool + `orfeo://alcance` resource, both derived from `build_scope` — two doors to one datum. Offered **always**, even to someone who reaches no write: the tool set says what you *can* do and stays silent about the rest, and silence is how a limit ends up being discovered by failing.
  The gateway now routes `/api/v1/mcp/`, which it deliberately did not before — leaving "one catalogue and one gate for the integrated assistant and for external MCP clients" half-done. It is safe to expose **because the write gate no longer depends on the client**: invoking a write returns a signed proposal and executes nothing. A new `shared/tests/` guardian forbids any tool from pointing at the MCP or assistant surface (recursion + gate bypass), which until now was impossible only because those paths 404'd.
  My own negative control ("forging the header grants nothing") **survived** re-reading the header — it passed for an unrelated reason. Replaced with a structural AST check, inspecting the tree and not the text, because the files are full of comments naming the header to explain its removal and a grep would have vouched for them.
  *Why*: a legitimate limit discovered only by failing wastes two people's time and looks like a breakdown — *Impact*: `/api/v1/mcp/` is routed through the gateway; `MCP_USER_PERMISSIONS`/`MCP_USER_ROLES` no longer exist; rebuild mcp-server and api-gateway.

- `feat(mcp)`: **instruments and physical archive — the catalogue closes at 45 tools**
  5 new read-only tools (plan block 8): `consultar_serie`, `consultar_retencion`, `ubicacion_expediente`, `listar_prestamos`, `reporte_radicacion`. The catalogue reaches **45 tools, 16 of them writes**, and all 45 cross-check against their endpoint's gate.
  Two descriptions state what the tool is *not*: `consultar_retencion` is a computation over a series and a closing date, not the expediente's recorded disposition; `reporte_radicacion` returns aggregates only — no tracking number, subject or sender — so a count is never presented as an inventory. `ubicacion_expediente` says where an expediente is, not what it holds (the backend deliberately skips no-read-up there: custody management is orthogonal to read clearance).
  `consultar_retencion` is the first GET with a required non-path argument; a test now pins series-in-path, date-in-query and no body, verified by mutation.
  *Why*: these are the lookups the write blocks depend on — typing a radicado without being able to read the series' retention is signing blind — *Impact*: catalogue 40 → 45 tools, no new writes.

- `feat(mcp)`: **the reply cycle — precedents, draft and registration — and a 422 that stopped being discovered by failing**
  10 new tools (plan block 7): `buscar_antecedentes`, `redactar_con_antecedentes` (text grounded in the archive, **with citations**, writing nothing), `listar_respuestas`, `crear_respuesta_vinculada`, `listar_plantillas`, `listar_borradores`, `crear_borrador`, `editar_borrador`, `aprobar_borrador`, `radicar_borrador`. The catalogue reaches **40 tools, 16 of them writes**. The draft is what lets the assistant **propose without committing**: no tracking number, not in the index, edited or abandoned without consequence — only `radicar_borrador` consumes the immutable number.
  `buscar_antecedentes` takes the subject or the reference tracking number, never both and never neither. Announced as two plain optionals, the model sends both and gets a 422 **after** making the person wait; declared with `exactly_one_of`, `coerce_args` rejects it before the request leaves. Its own invariant guards the declaration, because a group naming a non-existent parameter would reject every call to the tool.
  Also declared rather than hidden: `POST /borradores/{id}/radicar` does **not** require the draft to be approved, so `aprobar_borrador` is described as a procedural marker and not as a barrier.
  *Why*: "draft me the reply with precedents" was part of the end-to-end success criterion — *Impact*: catalogue 30 → 40 tools; rebuild the mcp-server image.

- `fix(workflow,mcp)`: **returning and rolling back a radicado required no permission at all**, and the workflow block was the one missing to close a trámite end to end — `POST /workflows/{id}/devolver` and `POST /workflows/{id}/rollback` had **no** `require_permission`, while their three siblings in the same router — `assign`, `transfer` and `complete` — require `PERM_TRAMITAR` over the same radicado. Same file, same object, only some gated: omission, not design, and they are the positive control. Any authenticated user in the tenant could pull a radicado out of the dependencia handling it and send it back to origin, or undo someone else's last assignment. **And a third hole, this one documented as a decision.** In `execute_transaction`, a type with `requires_permission = NULL` was "open": it executed with no permission whatsoever, and a test asserted exactly that — so it is changed by **declaring** the change, not by deleting the test. The reason: `transaction_types` is a tenant catalogue, so a row with an empty column — seeded by a future migration, or by someone with database access — was executable by anyone. Leaving a field blank cannot be the easiest way to open a door. It now falls back to the `PERM_TRAMITAR` floor. **Nothing observable changes today**: all 12 seeded types declare their own. The floor lives in the service rather than as a route gate because the permission here is PER TYPE: requiring `PERM_TRAMITAR` for all of them would break someone who holds `PERM_VOBO` to sign off and nothing else. **8 new tools** (block 6, the one that makes closing a trámite end to end possible, where previously there was not a single distribution tool): `pendientes_equipo`, `tipos_transaccion`, `hoja_ruta_radicado`, `paso_actual`, `asignar_radicado`, `trasladar_radicado`, `completar_paso` and `ejecutar_transaccion`. The catalogue now stands at **30 tools, 11 writes**. `completar_paso` names its parameter for what it is — `step_id`, the id of the STEP, not of the radicado — instead of inheriting the common one: that route hangs off a different entity than the rest of the router, and it was the most likely mistake in the whole block. **An exception that had to be declared rather than skipped.** `ejecutar_transaccion` resolves its permission at runtime, so there is no decorator to compare and the `test_permisos_declarados.py` guard flagged it as "requires a permission its endpoint does not ask for". Resolved with an explicit catalogue field (`permiso_en_runtime`) **plus a test of its own checking that the flag is not set on a route that does have a gate** — because that is how a legitimate exception eventually becomes the way to silence findings. — *Why*: exposing an operation over MCP makes it more reachable, so the loose gates it would amplify get closed first, not later — *Impact*: `devolver` and `rollback` require `PERM_TRAMITAR` (anyone without it now gets a 403 where the call used to go through). Rebuild the workflow-service and mcp-server images. Two false statements corrected in `docs/{es,en}/api.md`: `devolver`'s authorization and the "open type". Verified by mutation: removing each gate, restoring `NULL` to "open", and setting the runtime-permission flag where it does not belong each fail their own test.

- `feat(mcp)`: **three tools were offered to people who would get a 403 — and now something watches for it** — expanding the catalogue (plan blocks 4 and 5: from 9 tools to 22) surfaced the exact mirror image of the `RADI_CREAR` defect. `consultar_expediente`, `indice_expediente` and `consultar_fuid` declared **"no permission"** over endpoints gated with `USUA_PERM_EXPEDIENTE`. There, something usable was hidden; here, **something unusable was advertised** — worse in light of what this project committed to: the scope framework exists precisely so that nobody discovers a limit by failing, and a catalogue that promises what the backend denies turns it into a list of good intentions. The pre-existing invariant checks that the permission **exists** in the RBAC migrations — it would have caught `RADI_CREAR` and does not catch this: a `None` is a perfectly existing name. What was missing is comparing against **that specific endpoint's gate**, which needs both trees at once. `shared/tests/test_permisos_declarados.py` now does it, with one test per direction (under-declaring and over-declaring are two different failures with two different victims) and a third that measures **cross-match coverage**, without which the other two would be decorative: if path matching broke, everything would be skipped and they would stay green forever. Its twin, `test_rutas_mcp_alcanzables.py`, closes the gap that **already slipped through once**: bulk upload was built entirely against endpoints that existed while the gateway had no `/api/v1/batch/` in its routing table, so it returned `404 route_not_found`. A tool whose route is not routed gets offered, proposed, confirmed… and fails. Both live in `shared/tests/` because it is the only CI lane that sees the whole repository: the mcp-server container has no gateway source and the gateway's has no catalogue. Neither keeps a list of its own — both sides are derived from the code. **13 new tools.** Registration and classification: `consultar_por_numero` (the number is how people name it; the id is not on the stamp), `listar_anexos`, `consultar_disposicion`, `actualizar_radicado`, `tipificar_radicado`, `listar_tipos_documentales`. Expedientes: `crear_expediente`, `buscar_expedientes`, `vincular_radicado`, `actualizar_expediente`, `cerrar_expediente`, `consultar_foliado`, `hoja_ruta_expediente`. Parameters were verified against the real routers rather than the plan's table — and that cross-check is what surfaced both the typing gap and these three permissions. **Two deliberate exclusions, said out loud**: `actualizar_expediente` declares exactly `ExpedienteUpdate`'s three fields and no more, because that schema carries `extra="forbid"` and `status` was deliberately withdrawn there (it was an alternate route to closing, without audit or disposition); and **bulk linking** (up to 500 radicados) stays out for as long as its confirmation card cannot be read — a card saying "500 radicados" does not let anyone confirm knowingly, which is the only reason the card exists. **Two existing tests went red, for the right reason**: they passed on a wrong premise (the test user only had `PERM_RADI` and the catalogue asked for no more). Fixed by granting the real permission, not by relaxing the assertion. — *Why*: offering a capability the backend will deny wastes the person's time and turns a legitimate limit into an apparent malfunction — the very defect this plan set out to correct, committed inside the plan itself — *Impact*: the catalogue goes from 9 to 22 tools (6 writes, all via signed proposal). `cerrar_expediente` is the only irreversible one admitted, at `crud >= 3`, because "archiving" was explicitly part of what was asked for. Rebuild the mcp-server image. Verified by mutation: returning a tool to "no permission" and declaring a non-existent permission each fail their own test; pointing a tool at an unrouted path fails its own.

- `feat(document,mcp)`: **a radicado could not be typed after registration — the document type only existed at creation time** — surfaced while verifying block 4 of the MCP plan against the real routers instead of trusting the plan's table. `doc_class` — the document type — **was only set when creating the radicado** and in bulk import; `PATCH /api/v1/documents/{id}` accepted `subject`, `dest_dept`, `observations`, `status` and `pages`, and the type was not among them. **No endpoint did it, by this route or any other.** A document mis-typed at the counter — or registered untyped, which the schema allows — stayed that way forever, and with it the TRD series governing its retention and final disposition (Ley 594/2000 art. 24). It was not a deliberate restriction; it was a hole. It also explains why the frontend only suggests a type **at registration** (`TrdSuggestChips`): afterwards there was nowhere to send the correction. **Re-typing re-validates the metadata against the new type's template** — the heart of the fix, not an extra: without it, re-typing would have been an `UPDATE doc_class` leaving the previous type's variable fields dangling, and the radicado would have gone on claiming conformance to a template that was no longer its own. It uses **the same check `create` performs**, deliberately: a re-typed radicado must be indistinguishable from one registered that way from the start.
